Several church manuals or directories of public worship, and discipline have come down to us from the first centuries in different languages. They claim directly or indirectly apostolic origin and authority, but are post-apostolic and justly excluded from the canon. They give us important information on the ecclesiastical laws, morals, and customs of the ante-Nicene age.

  1. The Teaching of the Twelve Apostles is the oldest and simplest church manual, of Jewish Christian (Palestinian or Syrian) origin, from the end of the first century, known to the Greek fathers, but only recently discovered and published by Bryennios (1883). It contains in 16 chapters (1) a summary of moral instruction based on the Decalogue and the royal commandment of love to God and man, in the parabolic form of two ways, the way of life and the way of death; (2) directions on the celebration of baptism and the eucharist with the agape; (3) directions on discipline and the offices of apostles (i.e. travelling evangelists), prophets, teachers, bishops (i.e. presbysters), and deacons; (4) an exhortation to watchfulness in view of the coming of the Lord and the resurrection of the saints. A very remarkable book. Its substance survived in the seventh book of the Apostolical Constitutions.

  2. The Ecclesiastical Canons of the holy apostles or Apostolical Church Order, of Egyptian origin, probably of the third century. An expansion of the former in the shape of a fictitious dialogue of the apostles, first published in Greek by Bickell (1843), and then also in Coptic and Syriac. It contains ordinances of the apostles on morals, worship, and discipline.

  3. The Apostolical Constitutions, the most complete and important Church Manual. It is, in form, a literary fiction, professing to be a bequest of all the apostles, handed down through the Roman bishop Clement, or dictated to him. It begins with the words: "The apostles and elders, to all who among the nations have believed in the Lord Jesus Christ. Grace be with you, and peace." It contains, in eight books, a collection of moral exhortations, church laws and usages, and liturgical formularies which had gradually arisen in the various churches from the close of the first century, the time of the Roman Clement, downward, particularly in Jerusalem, Antioch, Alexandria, and Rome, partly on the authority of apostolic practice. These were at first orally transmitted; then committed to writing in different versions, like the creeds; and finally brought, by some unknown hand, into their present form. The first six books, which have a strongly Jewish-Christian tone, were composed, with the exception of some later interpolations, at the end of the third century, in Syria. The seventh book is an expansion of the Didache of the Twelve Apostles. The eighth book contains a liturgy, and, in an appendix, the apostolical canons. The collection of the three parts into one whole may be the work of the compiler of the eighth book. It is no doubt of Eastern authorship, for the church of Rome nowhere occupies a position of priority or supremacy.(147) The design was, to set forth the ecclesiastical life for laity and clergy, and to establish the episcopal theocracy. These constitutions were more used and consulted in the East than any work of the fathers, and were taken as the rule in matters of discipline, like the Holy Scriptures in matters of doctrine. Still the collection, as such, did not rise to formal legal authority, and the second Trullan council of 692 (known as quinisextum), rejected it for its heretical interpolations, while the same council acknowledged the Apostolical Canons.(148)

The "Apostolical Canons" consist of brief church rules or prescriptions, in some copies eighty-five in number, in others fifty, and pretend to be of apostolic origin, being drawn up by Clement of Rome from the directions of the apostles, who in several places speak in the first person. They are incorporated in the "Constitutions" as an appendix to the eighth book, but are found also by themselves, in Greek, Syriac, Aethiopic, and Arabic manuscripts. Their contents are borrowed partly from the Scriptures, especially the Pastoral Epistles, partly from tradition, and partly from the decrees of early councils at Antioch, Neo-Caesarea, Nicaea, Laodicea, &c. (but probably not Chalcedon, 451). They are, therefore, evidently of gradual growth, and were collected either after the middle of the fourth century,(149) or not till the latter part of the fifth,(150) by some unknown hand, probably also in Syria. They are designed to furnish a complete system of discipline for the clergy. Of the laity they say scarcely a word. The eighty-fifth and last canon settles the canon of the Scripture, but reckons among the New Testament books two epistles of Clement and the genuine books of the pseudo-Apostolic Constitutions.

The Greek church, at the Trullan council of 692, adopted the whole collection of eighty-five canons as authentic and binding, and John of Damascus placed it even on a parallel with the epistles of the apostle Paul, thus showing that he had no sense of the infinite superiority of the inspired writings. The Latin church rejected it at first, but subsequently decided for the smaller collection of fifty canons, which Dionysus Exiguus about the year 500 translated from a Greek manuscript.

§ 57. Church Discipline.

I. Several Tracts of Tertullian (especially De Poenitentia). The Philosophumena of Hippolytus (l. IX.). The Epistles of Cyprian, and his work De Lapsis. The Epistolae Canonicae of Dionysius of Alex., Gregory Thaumaturgus (about 260), and Peter of Alex. (about 306), collected in Routh’s Reliquiae Sacrae, tom. III., 2nd ed. The Constit. Apost. II. 16, 21–24. The Canons of the councils of Elvira, Arelate, Ancyra, Neo-Caesarea, and Nicaea, between 306 and 325 (in the Collections of Councils, and in Routh’s Reliq. Sacr. tom. IV.).

  1. Morinus: De Disciplina in administratione sacram poenitentiae, Par. 1651 (Venet. 1702).

Marshall: Penitential Discipline of the Primitive Church. Lond. 1714 (new ed. 1844).

Fr. Frank: Die Bussdisciplin der Kirche bis zum 7 Jahrh. Mainz. 1868.

On the discipline of the Montanists, see Bonwetsch: Die Geschichte des Montanismus (1881), pp. 108–118.

The ancient church was distinguished for strict discipline. Previous to Constantine the Great, this discipline rested on purely moral sanctions, and had nothing to do with civil constraints and punishments. A person might be expelled from one congregation without the least social injury. But the more powerful the church became, the more serious were the consequences of her censures, and when she was united with the state, ecclesiastical offenses were punished as offenses against the state, in extreme cases even with death. The church always abhorred blood ("ecclesia non sitit sanguiem"), but she handed the offender over to the civil government to be dealt with according to law. The worst offenders for many centuries were heretics or teachers of false doctrine.

The object of discipline was, on the one hand, the dignity and purity of the church, on the other, the spiritual welfare of the offender; punishment being designed to be also correction. The extreme penalty was excommunication, or exclusion from all the rights and privileges of the faithful. This was inflicted for heresy and schism, and all gross crimes, such as, theft, murder, adultery, blasphemy, and the denial of Christ in persecution. After Tertullian, these and like offences incompatible with the regenerate state, were classed as mortal sins,(151) in distinction from venial sins or sins of weakness.(152)

Persons thus excluded passed into the class of penitents,(153) and could attend only the catechumen worship. Before they could be re-admitted to the fellowship of the church, they were required to pass through a process like that of the catechumens, only still more severe, and to prove the sincerity of their penitence by the absence from all pleasures, from ornament in dress, and from nuptial intercourse, by confession, frequent prayer, fasting, almsgiving, and other good works. Under pain of a troubled conscience and of separation from the only saving church, they readily submitted to the severest penances. The church teachers did not neglect, indeed, to inculcate the penitent spirit and the contrition of the heart is the main thing. Yet many of them laid too great stress on certain outward exercises. Tertullian conceived the entire church penance as a "satisfaction" paid to God. This view could easily obscure to a dangerous degree the all-sufficient merit of Christ, and lead to that self-righteousness against which the Reformation raised so loud a voice.

The time and the particular form of the penances, in the second century, was left as yet to the discretion of the several ministers and churches. Not till the end of the third century was a rigorous and fixed system of penitential discipline established, and then this could hardly maintain itself a century. Though originating in deep moral earnestness, and designed only for good, it was not fitted to promote the genuine spirit of repentance. Too much formality and legal constraint always deadens the spirit, instead of supporting and regulating it. This disciplinary formalism first appears, as already familiar, in the council of Ancyra, about the year 314.(154)

Classes of Penitents.

The penitents were distributed into four classes:—

  1. The weepers,(155) who prostrated themselves at the church doors in mourning garments and implored restoration from the clergy and the people.

  2. The hearers,(156) who, like the catechumens called by the same name, were allowed to hear the Scripture lessons and the sermon.

  3. The kneelers,(157) who attended the public prayers, but only in the kneeling posture.

  4. The standers,(158) who could take part in the whole worship standing, but were still excluded from the communion.

Those classes answer to the four stages of penance.(159) The course of penance was usually three or four years long, but, like the catechetical preparation, could be shortened according to circumstances, or extended to the day of death. In the East there were special penitential presbyters,(160) intrusted with the oversight of the penitential discipline.

Restoration.

After the fulfilment of this probation came the act of reconciliation.(161) The penitent made a public confession of sin, received absolution by the laying on of hands of the minister, and precatory or optative benediction,(162) was again greeted by the congregation with the brotherly kiss, and admitted to the celebration of the communion. For the ministry alone was he for ever disqualified. Cyprian and Firmilian, however, guard against the view, that the priestly absolution of hypocritical penitents is unconditional and infallible, and can forestall the judgment of God.(163)

Two Parties.

In reference to the propriety of any restoration in certain cases, there was an important difference of sentiment, which gave rise to several schisms. All agreed that the church punishment could not forestall the judgment of God at the last day, but was merely temporal, and looked to the repentance and conversion of the subject. But it was a question whether the church should restore even the grossest offender on his confession of sorrow, or should, under certain circumstances leave him to the judgment of God. The strict, puritanic party, to which the Montanists, the Novatians, and the Donatists belonged, and, for a time, the whole African and Spanish Church, took ground against the restoration of those who had forfeited the grace of baptism by a mortal sin, especially by denial of Christ; since, otherwise, the church would lose her characteristic holiness, and encourage loose morality. The moderate party, which prevailed in the East, in Egypt, and especially in Rome, and was so far the catholic party, held the principle that the church should refuse absolution and communion, at least on the death-bed, to no penitent sinner. Paul himself restored the Corinthian offender.(164)

The point here in question was of great practical moment in the times of persecution, when hundreds and thousands renounced their faith through weakness, but as soon as the danger was passed, pleaded for readmission into the church, and were very often supported in their plea by the potent intercessions of the martyrs and confessors, and their libelli pacis. The principle was: necessity knows no law. A mitigation of the penitential discipline seemed in such cases justified by every consideration of charity and policy. So great was the number of the lapsed in the Decian persecution, that even Cyprian found himself compelled to relinquish his former rigoristic views, all the more because he held that out of the visible church there was no salvation.

The strict party were zealous for the holiness of God; the moderate, for his grace. The former would not go beyond the revealed forgiveness of sins by baptism, and were content with urging the lapsed to repentance, without offering them hope of absolution in this life. The latter refused to limit the mercy of God and expose the sinner to despair. The former were carried away with an ideal of the church which cannot be realized till the second coming of Christ; and while impelled to a fanatical separatism, they proved, in their own sects, the impossibility of an absolutely pure communion on earth. The others not rarely ran to the opposite extreme of a dangerous looseness, were quite too lenient, even towards mortal sins, and sapped the earnestness of the Christian morality.

It is remarkable that the lax penitential discipline had its chief support from the end of the second century, in the Roman church. Tertullian assails that church for this with bitter mockery. Hippolytus, soon after him, does the same; for, though no Montanist, he was zealous for strict discipline. According to his statement (in the ninth book of his Philosophumena), evidently made from fact, the pope Callistus, whom a later age stamped a saint because it knew little of him, admitted bigami and trigami to ordination, maintained that a bishop could not be deposed, even though he had committed a mortal sin, and appealed for his view to Rom. 14:4, to the parable of the tares and the wheat, Matt. 13:30, and, above all, to the ark of Noah, which was a symbol of the church, and which contained both clean and unclean animals, even dogs and wolves. In short, he considered no sin too great to be loosed by the power of the keys in the church. And this continued to be the view of his successors.

But here we perceive, also, how the looser practice in regard to penance was connected with the interest of the hierarchy. It favored the power of the priesthood, which claimed for itself the right of absolution; it was at the same time matter of worldly policy; it promoted the external spread of the church, though at the expense of the moral integrity of her membership, and facilitated both her subsequent union with the state and her hopeless confusion with the world. No wonder the church of Rome, in this point, as in others, triumphed at last over all opposition.

§ 58. Church Schisms.

I. On the Schism of Hippolytus-. The Philosophumena of Hippol. lib. IX. (ed. Miller, Oxf. 1851, better by Duncker and Schneidewin, Gött. 1859), and the monographs on Hippolytus, by Bunsen, Döllinger, Wordsworth, Jacobi, and others (which will be noticed in chapter XIII. § 183).

  1. On the Schism of Felicissimus: Cyprian: Epist. 38–40, 42, 55.

  2. On the Novatian Schism: Hippol.: Philosoph. 1 IX. Cypr.: Epist. 41–52; and the Epistles of Cornelius of Rome, and Dionys. of Alex., in Euseb. H. E., VI. 43–45; VII. 8. Comp. Lit. in § 200.

  3. On the Meletian Schism: Documents in Latin translation in Maffei: Osservationi Letterarie, Verona, 1738, tom. III p. 11 sqq., and the Greek fragments from the Liber de poenitentia of Peter of Alexandria in Routh: Reliquicae Sacr. vol. II. pp. 21–51. Epiphan.: Haer. 68 (favorable to Meletius); Athanas.: Apol. contra Arianos, § 59; and after him, Socr, Sozom., and Theod. (very unfavorable to Meletius).

Out of this controversy on the restoration of the lapsed, proceeded four schisms during the third century; two in Rome, one in North Africa, and one in Egypt. Montanism, too, was in a measure connected with the question of penitential discipline, but extended also to several other points of Christian life, and will be discussed in a separate chapter.

I. The Roman schism of Hippolytus. This has recently been brought to the light by the discovery of his Philosophumena (1851). Hippolytus was a worthy disciple of Irenaeus, and the most learned and zealous divine in Rome, during the pontificates of Zephyrinus (202–217), and Callistus (217–222). He died a martyr in 235 or 236. He was an advocate of strict views on discipline in opposition to the latitudinarian practice which we have described in the previous section. He gives a most unfavorable account of the antecedents of Callistus, and charges him and his predecessor with the patripassian heresy. The difference, therefore, was doctrinal as well as disciplinarian. It seems to have led to mutual excommunication and a temporary schism, which lasted till a.d. 235. Hippolytus ranks himself with the successors of the apostles, and seems to have been bishop of Portus, the port of Rome (according to later Latin tradition), or bishop of Rome (according to Greek writers). If bishop of Rome, he was the first schismatic pope, and forerunner of Novatianus, who was ordained anti pope in 251.(165) But the Roman Church must have forgotten or forgiven his schism, for she numbers him among her saints and martyrs, and celebrates his memory on the twenty-second of August. Prudentius, the spanish poet, represents him as a Roman presbyter, who first took part in the Novatian schism, then returned to the Catholic church, and was torn to pieces by wild horses at Ostia on account of his faith. The remembrance of the schism was lost in the glory of his supposed or real martyrdom. According to the chronological catalogue of Popes from a.d. 354, a "presbyter" Hippolytus, together with the Roman bishop Pontianus, the successor of Callistus, was banished from Rome in the reign of Alexander Severus (235), to the mines of Sardinia.(166)

  1. The schism of Felicississimus, at Carthage, about the year 250, originated in the personal dissatisfaction of five presbyters with the hasty and irregular election of Cyprian to the bishopric, by the voice of the congregation, very soon after his baptism, a.d. 248. At the head of this opposition party stood the presbyter Novatus, an unprincipled ecclesiastical demagogue, of restless, insubordinate spirit and notorious character,(167) and the deacon Felicissimus, whom Novatus ordained, without the permission or knowledge of Cyprian, therefore illegally, whether with his own hands or through those of foreign bishops. The controversy cannot, however, from this circumstance, be construed, as it is by Neander and others, into a presbyterial reaction against episcopal autocracy. For the opponents themselves afterwards chose a bishop in the person of Fortunatus. The Novatians and the Meletians likewise had the episcopal form of organization, though doubtless with many irregularities in the ordination.

After the outbreak of the Decian persecution this personal rivalry received fresh nourishment and new importance from the question of discipline. Cyprian originally held Tertullian’s principles, and utterly opposed the restoration of the lapsed, till further examination changed his views. Yet, so great was the multitude of the fallen, that he allowed an exception in periculo mortis. His opponents still saw even in this position an unchristian severity, least of all becoming him, who, as they misrepresented him, fled from his post for fear of death. They gained the powerful voice of the confessors, who in the face of their own martyrdom freely gave their peace-bills to the lapsed. A regular trade was carried on in these indulgences. An arrogant confessor, Lucian, wrote to Cyprian in the name of the rest, that he granted restoration to all apostates, and begged him to make this known to the other bishops. We can easily understand how this lenity from those who stood in the fire, might take more with the people than the strictness of the bishop, who had secured himself. The church of Novatus and Felicissimus was a resort of all the careless lapsi. Felicissimus set himself also against a visitation of churches and a collection for the poor, which Cyprian ordered during his exile.

When the bishop returned, after Easter, 251, he held a council at Carthage, which, though it condemned the party of Felicissimus, took a middle course on the point in dispute. It sought to preserve the integrity of discipline, yet at the same time to secure the fallen against despair. It therefore decided for the restoration of those who proved themselves truly penitent, but against restoring the careless, who asked the communion merely from fear of death. Cyprian afterwards, when the persecution was renewed, under Gallus, abolished even this limitation. He was thus, of course, not entirely consistent, but gradually accommodated his principles to circumstances and to the practice of the Roman church.(168) His antagonists elected their bishop, indeed, but were shortly compelled to yield to the united force of the African and Roman churches, especially as they had no moral earnestness at the bottom of their cause.

His conflict with this schismatical movement strengthened Cyprian’s episcopal authority, and led him in his doctrine of the unity of the church to the principle of absolute exclusiveness.

  1. The Novatian schism in Rome was prepared by the controversy already alluded to between Hippolytus and Callistus. It broke out soon after the African schism, and, like it, in consequence of an election of bishop. But in this case the opposition advocated the strict discipline against the lenient practice of the dominant church. The Novatianists(169) considered themselves the only pure communion,(170) and unchurched all churches which defiled themselves by re-admitting the lapsed, or any other gross offenders. They went much farther than Cyprian, even as far as the later Donatists. They admitted the possibility of mercy for a mortal sinner, but denied the power and the right of the church to decide upon it, and to prevent, by absolution, the judgment of God upon such offenders. They also, like Cyprian, rejected heretical baptism, and baptized all who came over to them from other communions not just so rigid as themselves.

At the head of this party stood the Roman presbyter Novatian,(171) an earnest, learned, but gloomy man, who had come to faith through severe demoniacal disease and inward struggles. He fell out with Cornelius, who, after the Decian persecution in 251, was nominated bishop of Rome, and at once, to the grief of many, showed great indulgence towards the lapsed. Among his adherents the above-named Novatus of Carthage was particularly busy, either from a mere spirit of opposition to existing authority, or from having changed his former lax principles on his removal to Rome. Novatian, against his will, was chosen bishop by the opposition. Cornelius excommunicated him. Both parties courted the recognition of the churches abroad. Fabian, bishop of Antioch, sympathized with the rigorists. Dionysius of Alexandria, on the contrary, accused them of blaspheming the most gracious Lord Jesus Christ, by calling him unmerciful. And especially Cyprian, from his zeal for ecclesiastical unity and his aversion to Novatus, took sides with Cornelius, whom he regarded the legitimate bishop of Rome.

In spite of this strong opposition the Novatian sect, by virtue of its moral earnestness, propagated itself in various provinces of the West and the East down to the sixth century. In Phrygia it combined with the remnants of the Montanists. The council of Nicaea recognized its ordination, and endeavored, without success, to reconcile it with the Catholic church. Constantine, at first dealt mildly with the Novatians, but afterwards prohibited them to worship in public and ordered their books to be burnt.

  1. The Meletian schism in Egypt arose in the Diocletian persecution, about 305, and lasted more than a century, but, owing to the contradictory character of our accounts, it is not so well understood. It was occasioned by Meletius, bishop of Lycopolis in Thebais, who, according to one statement, from zeal for strict discipline, according to another, from sheer arrogance, rebelled against his metropolitan, Peter of Alexandria (martyred in 311), and during his absence encroached upon his diocese with ordinations, excommunications, and the like. Peter warned his people against him, and, on returning from his flight, deposed him as a disturber of the peace of the church. But the controversy continued, and spread over all Egypt. The council of Nicaea endeavored, by recognizing the ordination of the twenty-nine Meletian bishops, and by other compromise measures, to heal the division; but to no purpose. The Meletians afterwards made common cause with the Arians.

The Donatist schism, which was more formidable than any of those mentioned, likewise grew out of the Diocletian persecution, but belongs more to the next period.

(44; original 161): Bishop Lightfoot (l.c. p. 194) thus states the question with his own answer: "The episcopate was formed, not out of the apostolic order by localization, but out of the presbyterial by elevation; and the title, which originally was common to all, came at length to be appropriated to the chief among them."

(55; original 172): The reading is obscure and disputed. The Alexandrian MS. reads: ejpinomhvn, the Constantinopolitan: ejpidomhvn (both have EIII-OMHN). The former word is rare (from or from nevmw or fromnovmo") is not found in the dictionaries; and hence various emendations have been proposed, as avponomhvn (Junius), ejpidochvn (Bryennios), ejpibolhvn (von Gebhardt and Harnack), ejpimonhvn (Bunsen, Lightfoot), ejpitrophvn (Hilgenfeld), ejpiloghvn, ejpinomivan, ejpistolhvn, ejpitaghvn, e[ti novmon. Rothe (Anfänge, p. 374) ingeniously translates ejpinomhvn " testamentary disposition" (testamentarische Verfügung = ejpinomiv" ,an after-enactment, a codicil), and identifies it with the deuvterai diatavxei" of the fragment of Irenaeus. But this is rejected by the latest editors as untenable. Lightfoot (with Bunsen) reads ejpimonhvn, permanence (not " life-tenure,"as Bunsen rendered it). The drift of the passage, however, does not so much depend upon the meaning of this word as upon the question whether the apostles, or the congregational officers are the grammatical subjects of the following verb, koimhqw'sin.

(56; original 173): See also Gebhardt and Harnack (presbyteri et diaconi illi, quos apostoli ipsi constituerunt), the Roman Catholic editor Funk ("koimhqw'sin, sc. episcopi et diaconi de quorum successione Clemens agit"), and Bishop Lightfoot ("the first generation of presbyters appointed by the apostles themselves"). (Comp. also on this whole passage Lightfoot, Philippians, p. 203, where he refutes Rothe’s interpretation; Baur Ursprung des Episcopats, p. 53; Ewald, Gesch. des Volkes Israel, VII. 300; Ritschl, Altkath. K. 358 and 413, and Ilgenfeld, Apost. Väter, 70.

(57; original 174): Hence Rothe traces the institution to John. And Bishop Lightfoot (Philippians, p. 204) is inclined to this view: "Asia Minor was the nurse, if not the mother of episcopacy in the Gentile churches. So important an institution, developed in a Christian community, of which St. John was the living centre and guide, could hardly, have grown up without his sanction: and early tradition very distinctly connects his name with the appointment of bishops in these parts." He repeats the same view more confidently in his Ignat. and Polyc., I. 377.

(69; original 186): The philanthropic and financial aspect of episcopacy has been brought out very fully by Hatch, in his Bampton Lectures on The Organization of the Early Christian Churches, Lect. II.

(70; original 187): The question of the genuineness will be discussed in § 165. Cureton (1845) Bunsen, Lipsius, and others accept the Syriac version as the original form of the Ignatian epistles, and regard even the short Greek text as corrupt, but yet as dating from the middle of the second century. Rothe, Hefele, Schaff (first ed.), Düsterdieck, Uhlhorn, Zahn, Harnack, defend the genuineness of the shorter Greek recension. The larger Greek recension is universally given up as spurious. The origin of the hierarchical system is obscured by pious frauds. See below, § 164 and 165.

(71; original 188): In the Syriac Ep. to Polycarp, the word bishop occurs four times; in the Syriac Ep. to the Ephesians, God is blessed for having given them such a bishop as Onesimus. In the shorter Greek Ep. to Polycarp episcopacy is mentioned in the salutation, and in three of the eight chapters (ch. 5 twice, ch. 6 twice, ch. 8 once). In the 21 chapters of the Greek Ep. to the Ephesians, the word bishop occurs thirteen times, presbyter three times, and deacon once (in the first six chapters, and ch. 21). In the Greek Trallians, the bishop appears nine times; in the Magnesians, eleven times; in the Philadelphians, eight times; in the Smynaeans, nine times. Thus in the three Syriac Epistles the bishop is mentioned but six times; in the seven shorter Greek Epistles about fifty times; but one of the strongest passages is found in the Syriac Epistle to Polycarp (ch. 5. and 6.).

(84; original 201): Comp. Adv. Haer. III. 3, §1, 2; 4, 1; IV. 33, §8. I remember what great stress the late Dr. Posey, when I saw him at Oxford in 1844, laid on the testimony of Irenaeus for the doctrine of an unbroken episcopal succession, as the indispensable mark of a genuine Catholic church; while he ignored the simultaneous growth of the primacy, which a year afterwards carried his friend, J. H. Newman, over to the church of Rome. The New Testament is the only safe guide and ultimate standard in all matters of faith and discipline. The teaching of Irenaeus on episcopacy is well set forth by Lightfoot (l.c. p. 237): Irenaeus followed Ignatius after an interval of about two generations. With the altered circumstances of the Church, the aspect of the episcopal office has also undergone a change. The religious atmosphere is now charged with heretical speculations of all kinds. Amidst the competition of rival teachers. all eagerly bidding for support, the perplexed believer asks for some decisive test by which he may try the claims of disputants. To this question Irenaeeus supplies an answer. ’If you wish,’ he argues, ’to ascertain the doctrine of the Apostles, apply to the Church of the Apostles.’ In the succession of bishops tracing their descent from the primitive age and appointed by the Apostles themselves, you have a guarantee for the transmission of the pure faith, which no isolated, upstart, self-constituted teacher can furnish. There is the Church of Rome for instance, whose episcopal pedigree is perfect in all its links, and whose earliest bishops, Linus and Clement, associated with the Apostles themselves: there is the Church of Smyrna again, whose bishop Polycarp, the disciple of St. John, died only the other day. Thus the episcopate is regarded now not so much as the centre of ecclesiastical unity, but rather as the depositary of apostolic tradition."

(97; original 214): The country bishops (cwrepivskopoi) appear first in the councils of Ancyra and Neo-Caesarea, 314, and again in the Council of Nicaea. They continued to exist in the East till the 9th century, when they were superseded by the exarchs (e[xarcoi) In the West, the chorepiscopi performed regular episcopal functions, without proper subordination to the diocesans, and hence excited jealousy and hostility till the office was abolished under Charlemagne, and continued only as a title of various cathedral dignitaries. See Haddan in Smith & Cheetham Dict. Chr. Ant. I. 354, and the authorities quoted there

(105; original 222): The famous Passage, Adv. Haer. iii. §2, is only extant in Latin, and of disputed interpretation: "Ad hanc enim ecclesiam propter potentiorem (according to Massuet’s conjecture: potiorem) principalitatem necesse est omnem convenire ecclesia, hoc est, eos qui sunt undique fideles, in qua semper ab his, qui sunt undique, conservata est ab apostolis traditio." In the original Greek it probably read: Prov" tauvthn ga;r th;n ejkklhsivan dia; th;n iJkanwtevran prwtei'an sumbaivnein (or, in the local sense, sunevrcesqai) dei' (according to others: ajnavgkh, natural necessity) pa'san th;n ejkklhsivan, etc. The stress lies on principalitas, which stands probably for prwteiva (so Thiersch and Gieseler). Comp. Iren. IV. 38, 3, where prwteuvei is rendered principatitatem habet. Stieren and Ziegler (Irenaeus, 1871, p. 152), however, translate propter potentiorem principalitatem: oJia; th;n iJkanwtevran ajrcaiovthta, " on account of the higher antiquity."Comp. on the whole passage an essay by Thiersch in the " Studien und Kritiken" 1842, 512 sqq.; Gieseler I. 1. p. 214 (§ 51); Schneemann: Sancti Irenaei de ecclesia Romanae principatu testimonium commentatum et defensum, Freiburg i. B. 1870, and Langen, l.c. p. 170 sqq. Langen (who is an Old Catholic of the Döllinger school) explains: " Die potior principalitas bezeichnet den Vorrang, welchen die Kirche der Hauptptstadt als solche vor alten übrigen Kirchen besass ... die Hauptstadt war das Centrum des damaligen Weltverkehrs, und in Folge dessen der Sammelplats von Christen aller Art."He defends the local sense of convenire by parallel passages from Herveus of Bordeaux and Hugo Eterianus (p. 172 sq.). But the moral sense (to agree)seems more natural.

(109; original 226): Cardinal Newman says (Apologia, p. 407): "The see of Rome possessed no great mind in the whole period of persecution. Dean Stanley remarks (Christian Institutions, p. 241): "There have been occupants of the sees of Constantinople, Alexandria, and Canterbury who have produced more effect on the mind of Christendom by their utterances than any of the popes."

(111; original 228): Or at least the first appointed by Peter. Tertullian De Praescr. HaeR.C. 32 "Romanorum Clementem a Petro ordinatum." The Apost. Const. VII. 6 make Linus (Comp. 2 Tim. 4:21) the first bishop, appointed by Paul, Clement the next, appointed by Peter. According to Epiphanius (Haer. XXVII. 6) Clement was ordained by Peter, but did not enter upon his office till after the death of Linus and Anacletus.

(112; original 229): The Catalogue of Irenaeus (Adv. Haer. III. 3, 3) down to his own time (a.d. 177) is this: The apostles Peter and Paul, Linos, Anacletos, Clement, Evaristus, Alexander, Xystos, Telesphoros, who died gloriously as a martyr, Hyginos, Pios, Aniketos, Soter, Eleutheros, who then held "the inheritance of the episcopate in the twelfth place from the apostles." Irenaeus adds: "In this order and by this succession, the ecclesiastical tradition from the apostles and the preaching of the truth have come down to us."

(113; original 230): Langen (l. c .p. 100 sqq.) carries the line of Roman presbyter-bishops down to Alexander, and dates the monarchical constitution of the Roman church (i.e. the diocesan episcopacy) from the age of Trajan or Hadrian. Irenaeus (in Euseb. V. 27) calls the Roman bishops down to Anicetus (154) presbuvteroi.

(114; original 232): The Church of England retained the term "catholic" in the Creed, and the, ante-papal and anti-papal use of this; term ( = general, universal); while Luther in his Catechism, and the Moravian church (in her liturgy) substituted the word "Christian," and surrendered the use of "catholic" to the Roman Catholics. "Roman" is a sectarian term (in opposition to Greek Catholic and Evangelical Catholic).

(129; original 247): On the provincial councils of the Roman empire see Marquardt,Römische Staatsverwaltung, I. 365-377, and Hatch, l.c. p. 164 sqq. The deliberations were preceded by a sacrifice, and the president was called highpriest.

(130; original 248): That is, within the limits of the old Roman empire, as the orbis terrarum. There never was an absolutely universal council. Even the seven oecumenical Councils from 325 to 787 were confined to the empire, and poorly attended by Western bishops. The Roman Councils held after that time (down to the Vatican Council in 1870) claim to be oecumenical, but exclude the Greek and all evangelical churches.

(131; original 249): Comp. Acts 15:6, 7, 12, 13, 23, where the "brethren" are mentioned expressly, besides the apostles and elders, as members of the council, even at the final decision and in the pastoral letter. On the difference of reading, see vol. I. 505.

(132; original 250): Cyprian, Opera, p. 329, ed. Baluz. In the acts of this council, however (pp. 330-338), only the bishops appear as voters, from which some writers infer that the laity, and even the presbyters, had no votum decisium. But in several old councils the presbyters and deacons subscribed their names after those of the bishops; see Harduin, Coll. Conc. I. 250 and 266; Hefele I. 19.

(136; original 254): Epistolae formatae, gravmmata tetupwmevna.

(137; original 255): This policy was inaugurated by Constantine I. a.d. 326 (Cod. Theod. 16, 5, 1). He confined the privileges and immunities which, in 313, he had granted to Christians in his later enactments to "Catholicae legis observatoribus." He ratified the Nicene creed and exiled Arius (325), although he afterwards wavered and was baptized by a semi-Arian bishop (337). His immediate successors wavered likewise. But as a rule the Byzantine emperors recognized the decisions of councils in dogma and discipline, and discouraged and ultimately prohibited the formation of dissenting sects. The state can, of course, not prevent dissent as an individual opinion; it can only prohibit and punish the open profession. Full religious liberty requires separation of church and state.

(138; original 256): Hefele, Gams, and Dale decide in favor of this date against the superscription which puts it down to the period of the Council of Nicaea (324). The chief reason is that Hosius, bishop of Cordova, could not be, present in 324 when he was in the Orient, nor at any time after 307, when he joined the company of Constantine as one of his private councillors.

(139; original 257): "Placuit picturas in ecclesia esse non debere, ne quod colitur et adoratur in parietibus depingatur.""There shall be no pictures in the church, lest what is worshipped [saints] and adored [God and Christ] should be depicted on the walls."

(140; original 258): The last is the interpretation of the canon by DeRossi, in Roma sotteranea, Tom. I., p. 97, and Hefele, I. 170. But Dale (p. 292 sqq.) thinks that it was aimed against the idolatry of Christians.

(141; original 259): The best accounts of the Synod of Elvira are given by Ferdinand de Mendoza, De confirmando Concilio Illiberitano ad Clementem VIII., 1593 (reprinted in Mansi II. 57-397); Fr. Ant. Gonzalez, Collect. Can. Ecclesiae Hispaniae, Madrid, 1808, new ed. with Spanish version, 1849 (reprinted in Bruns, Bibl. Eccl. Tom. I. Pars II. 1 sqq.); Hefele, Conciliengesch. I. 148-192 (second ed., 1873; or 122 sqq., first ed.); Gams, Kirchengesch. von Spanien (1864), vol. II. 1-136; and Dale in his monograph on the Synod of Elvira, London, 1882.

(142; original 260): Concilium Arelatense, from Arelate or Arelatum Sextanorum, one of the chief Roman cities in South-Eastern Gaul, where Constantine at one time resided, and afterwards the West Gothic King Eurich. It was perhaps the seat of the first bishopric of Gaul, or second only to that of Lyons and Vienne. Several councils were held in that city, the second in 353 during the Arian controversy.

(143; original 261): Not 633, as McClintock & Strong’s "Cyclop" has it sub Arles.

(144; original 262): See Eus. H. E. x. 5; Mansi, II. 463-468; München, Das ersten Concil von Arles (in the "Bonner Zeitschrift für Philos. und kath. Theol.," No. 9, 26, 27), and Hefele I. 201-219 (2nd ed.).

(145; original 263): Hefele, vol. I. 222 sqq., gives the canons in Greek and German with explanation. He calls it a Synodus plenaria, i.e., a general council for the churches of Asia Minor and Syria. See also Mansi II. 514 sqq. Two Arian Synods were held at Ancyra in 358 and 375.

(146; original 264): See Hefele I. 242-251.

(147; original 265): Harnack (l.c. 266-268) identifies Pseudo-Clement with Pseudo-Ignatius and assigns him to the middle of the fourth century.

(148; original 266): Turrianus Bovius; and the eccentric Whiston regarded these pseudoapostolic Constitutions as a genuine work of the apostles; containing Christ’s teaching during the forty days between the Resurrection and Ascension. But Baronius, Bellarmin, and Petavius attached little weight to them, and the Protestant scholars, Daillé and Blondel, attacked and overthrew their genuineness and authority. The work is a gradual growth, with many repetitions, interpolations, and contradictions and anachronisms. James, who was beheaded (a.d. 44), is made to sit in council with Paul (VI. 14), but elsewhere is represented as dead (V. 7). The apostles condemn post-apostolic heresies and heretics (VI. 8), and appoint days of commemoration of their death (VIII. 33). Episcopacy is extravagantly extolled. P. de Lagarde says: (Rel juris Eccles. ant., Preface, p. IV.): "Communis vivorum doctorum fere omnium nunc invaluit opinio eas [constitutiones] saeculo tertio clam succrevisse et quum sex aliquando libris septimo et octavo auctas esse postea."

(149; original 267): As Bickell supposes. Beveridge put the collection in the third century.

(150; original 268): According to Daillé, Dr. von Drey, and Mejer.

(151; original 269): Peccata mortalia, or, ad mortem; after a rather arbitrary interpretation of 1 John 5:16. Tertullian gives seven mortal sins: Homocidium idololatria, fraus, negatio blasphemia. utique et moechia et. fornicatio et si qua alia violatio templi Dei. De pudic. c. 19, These he declares irremissibilia,horum ultra exoratur non erit Christus; that is, if thev be committed after baptism; for baptism washes, away all former guilt. Hence he counselled delay of baptism.

(152; original 270): Peccata, venialia.

(153; original 271): Poenitentes.

(154; original 272): Can. 4 sqq. See Hefele, Conciliengesch (second ed.) I. 225 sqq. Comp. also the fifth canon of Neocaesarea, and Hefele, p. 246.

(155; original 273): Prosklaivonte", flentes; also called ceimavzonte", hiemantes

(156; original 274): !Akrowvmenoi, audientes, or auditores. The fourteenth canon of Nicaea (Hefele I. 418) directs that "Catechumens who had fallen, should for three years be only hearers, but afterwards pray with the Catechumens."

(157; original 275): Gonuklivnonte", genuflectentes: also uJpopivptonte" , Substrati. The terra govnu klivnwnas designating a class of penitents occurs only in the 5th canon of the Council of Neocaesarea, held after 314 and before 325.

(158; original 276): Sunistavmenoi, consistentes.

(159; original 277): Provsklausi", fletus; ajkrovasi" auditus; uJpovptwsi", prostratio, humiliatio; suvstasi", consistentia. The last three classes are supposed to correspond to three classes of catechumens, but without good reason. There was only one class of catechumens, or at most two classes. See below, § 72.

(160; original 278): Presbuvteroi ejpi; th'" metanoiva", presbyteri poenitentiarii

(161; original 279): Reconciliatio.

(162; original 280): The declarative, and especially the direct indicative or judicial form of absolution seems to be of later origin.

(163; original 281): Cypr. Epist. LV., c. 15: "Neque enim prejudicamus Domino judicaturo, quominus si penitentiam plenam et justam peccatoris invenerit tunc ratum faciat, quod a nobis fuerit hic statutum. Si vero nos aliquis poenitentiae simulatione deluserit, Deus, cui non deridetur, et qui cor hominis intuetur, de his, quae nos minus perspeximus, judicet et servorum suorum sententiam Dominus mendet." Comp. the similar passages in Epist. LXXV. 4, and De Lapsi, c. 17. But if the church can err in imparting absolution to the unworthy, as Cyprian concedes, she can err also in withholding absolution and in passing sentence of excommunication.

(164; original 282): 1 Cor. 5:1 sqq. Comp. 2 Cor. 2:5 sqq.

(165; original 283): See the particulars in § 183, and in Döllinger’s Hippol. and Call., Engl. transl. by A. Plummer (1876), p. 92 sqq.

(166; original 284): See Mommsen, Über den Chronographen vom Jahr 354 (1850), Lipsius, Chronologie der Röm. Bischöfe, p. 40 sqq.; Döllinger, I.c. p. 332 sqq.; Jacobi in Herzog2 VI. 142 sqq.

(167; original 285): Cyprian charges him with terrible cruelties, such as robbing widows and orphans, gross abuse of his father, and of his wife even during her pregnancy; and says, that he was about to be arraigned for this and similar misconduct when the Decian persecution broke out. Ep. 49.

(168; original 286): In Ep. 52, Ad Antonianum, he tried to justify himself in regard to this change in his views.

(169; original 287): Novatiani, Novatianenses.

(170; original 288): Kaqaroiv.

(171; original 289): Eusebius and the Greeks call him Noouavto", and confound him with Novatus of Carthage. Dionysius of Alex., however, calls him Noouatianov".

  1. “Quid Christianis cum regibus ? aut quid episcopis cum palatio?”（“基督徒与君王有何相干？主教与宫廷又有何相干？”）↩︎

  2. 《驳塞尔苏》(Contra Cels.)，VIII. 68。参尼安德 (Neander) 的论述，第I卷，第129页（波士顿版）。↩︎

  3. 勒南 (Renan) 从世俗的观点考察教阶制度如何从原始的民主制度逐渐发展出来，称其为历史上“最深刻的变革”，也是三重权力让渡：首先，社团（会众）把权力交给办事机构或委员会（长老团）；随后，办事机构把权力交给其主席（主教），后者可以说：“Je suis le club”（“我就是社团”）；最后，各位主席又把权力交给教皇，奉他为普世而无谬误的主教；最后这一步在1870年的梵蒂冈会议 (Vatican Council) 上完成。见其《基督教会》(E’glise chrétienne)，第88页，以及《英国讲演集》(English Conferences，希伯特讲座 [Hibbert Lectures]，1880年)，第90页。↩︎

  4. 参《使徒行传》8:4；9:27；13:15；18:26、28；《罗马书》12:6；《哥林多前书》12:10、28；14:1–6、31。甚至在犹太会堂中，人们也享有教导的自由，长老可以请任何有声望的成员，甚至外来者，就所读的经课发表讲论（《路加福音》4:17；《使徒行传》17:2）。↩︎

  5. 《彼得前书》2:5、9；5:3；《启示录》1:6；5:10；20:6。见尼安德 (Neander)、莱特福特 (Lightfoot)、斯坦利 (Stanley) 等人的著作，以及本书第I卷，第486页及后。我再引哈奇 (Hatch) 的班普顿讲座《早期基督教会的组织》(The Organization of the Early Christian Churches，1881年)，第139页的一段话：“在较早的时代，信仰更为宏大。因为上帝的国乃是祭司的国。不仅宝座前的‘二十四位长老’，而且那无数成圣的灵魂，就是‘第二次的死在他们身上没有权柄’的人，也都是‘归于上帝的君王和祭司’。唯有在这一崇高的意义上，才能称基督徒为祭司。因为影儿已经过去，实体已经来到；基督教唯一的大祭司就是基督。”↩︎

  6. 《出埃及记》19:6。↩︎

  7. 《彼得前书》5:3。这里彼得告诫同作长老的人，不可辖制（κυριεύειν*）那 κλῆροι* 或 κληρονομία*，即主的分或产业，也就是分派给他们照管的群羊。参《申命记》4:20；9:29（七十士译本 [LXX]）。↩︎

  8. 参《以弗所书》4:11–13。↩︎

  9. 《罗马书》12:1；《腓立比书》2:17；《彼得前书》2:5；《希伯来书》13:16。↩︎

  10. 《希伯来书》13:10。托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas)、本格尔 (Bengel)、布莱克 (Bleek)、吕讷曼 (Lünemann)、里姆 (Riehm) 等人都是这样理解 θυσιαστήριον* 的。另有人将它解释为主的桌子，莱特福特 (Lightfoot，第263页) 则将它解释为聚集在一起共同敬拜的会众。↩︎

  11. 《致特拉勒人书》(Ad Trall.)，第7章：ὁ ἐντὸς θυσιαστηρίου ὢν καθαρός ἐστιν ὁ δὲ ἐκτὸς θυσιαστηρίου ὢν οὐ καθαρός ἐστιν: τουτέστιν, ὁ χωρὶς ἐπισκόπου καὶ πρεσβυτερίου καὶ διακόνου πράσσων τι, οὗτος οὐ καθαρός ἐστιν τῇ συνειδήσει*。冯克 (Funk) 版，第I卷，第208页。有些抄本省略了第二个分句，可能是因同尾漏抄 (homoeoteleuton) 所致。冯·格布哈特 (Von Gebhardt) 和哈纳克 (Harnack) 也在希腊文本中将它省略，却在拉丁文本中保留（qui extra attare est, non mundus est，“在祭坛之外的，是不洁净的”）。其中的 τουτέστιν* 显然要求有这个分句。↩︎

  12. 参第13章。见沙弗 (Schaff) 所编版本的注释，第206页。↩︎

  13. 《致哥林多人书》(Ad Cor.)，40：“大祭司受托承担他特有的事奉，祭司有为他们指定的职务，利未人也被赋予他们特有的服事。平信徒须受平信徒的规条约束（ὁ λαϊκὸς ἄνθρωπος τοῖς λαϊκοῖς προστάγμασιν δέδεται*）。”这段话既见于布吕恩尼奥斯 (Bryennios) 的文本，也见于较早的各版；没有充分理由像尼安德 (Neander) 和米尔曼 (Milman) 那样，怀疑它是为维护教阶制度的利益而增插的。莱特福特 (Lightfoot) 主教在其《罗马的圣革利免》(St. Clement of Rome)，第128页及下页，对此作了较为温和的解释。他说，这个类比并不涉及三级教职，因为革利免只知道两级（主教和执事）；而且，基督的大祭司职分与摩西制度的大祭司职分，在性质上全然不同，不受革利免在该章所详论的那些限制。↩︎

  14. Sacerdos（祭司），也称 summus sacerdos（大祭司；德尔图良 [Tertullian]，《论洗礼》[De Bapt.]，7），还有一次称为 pontifex maximus（最高祭司；《论贞洁》[De Pudic.]，1，似乎是讽刺地指罗马主教）；ordo sacerdotalis（祭司阶层；《劝守贞洁》[De Exhort. Cast.]，7）；ἱερεύς*，有时也用 ἀρχιερεύς*（《使徒宪章》[Apost. Const.]，II. 34、35、36、57；III. 9；VI. 15、18等）。希波吕托 (Hippolytus) 称自己的职分为 ἀρχιερατεία* 和 διδασκαλία*（《驳异端》[Ref. Haer.]，I，序言）。居普良 (Cyprian) 通常把 sacerdos 一词用于主教，并称他的同僚为 consacerdotales（同任祭司者）。↩︎

  15. Κλῆρος*，clerus；τάξις*，ordo, ordosacerdotalis（德尔图良 [Tertulli]，《劝守贞洁》[De Ehort. Cast.]，7），ordo eccelesiasticus 或 ecclesiae（教会阶层；《论一夫一妻制》[De Monog.]，11；《论偶像崇拜》[De Idolol.]，7）；κληρικοί*，clerici（教士）。以 clerus 表示教士，最早的例子或许见于德尔图良《论一夫一妻制》(De Monog.)，第12章：“Unde enim episcopi et clerus？”（“那么，主教和教士从何而来？”）又说：“Extollimur et inflamur adversus clerum。”（“我们自高自大，向教士趾高气扬。”）哲罗姆 (Jerome) 在《致尼波提安书》(Ad Nepotian.) 中这样解释 clerus 一词何以专用于教牧人员：“vel quia de sorte sunt Domini, vel quia ipse Dominus sors, id est, pars clericorum est。”（“或因他们属于主的分，或因主自己就是教士的分，也就是他们所得的产业。”）兼为修士的修会教士与在俗教士或堂区司铎之间的区别，则出现得晚得多（七或八世纪）。↩︎

  16. Λαός*，λαϊκοί*，plebs（平信徒）。在德尔图良 (Tertullian)、居普良 (Cyprian) 的著作和《使徒宪章》中，“平信徒”一词出现得非常频繁。居普良在250年提到一次“与主教、长老、执事、认信者，以及在逼迫中坚立的平信徒共同举行的会议”，见《书信》(Ep.) 30，致罗马人 (ad Rom)。↩︎

  17. 不过，我们偶尔也会见到范围稍广的用语。德尔图良 (Tertullian) 在《论一夫一妻制》(De Monog.)，第12章，将 ordo viduarum（寡妇阶层）列入 ordines ecclesiastici（教会各阶层）；甚至晚得多的哲罗姆 (Jerome，见《以赛亚书注释》[In Jesaiam]，第V卷，第19章18节) 也列举 quinque ecclesiae ordines, episcopos, presbyteros, diaconos, fideles, catechumenos，即教会的五个阶层：主教、长老、执事、信徒、慕道者。↩︎

  18. Πάροικοι*，παρεπίδημοι*；《以弗所书》2:19；《彼得前书》2:11。↩︎

  19. 或堂区，παροικία*。↩︎

  20. 《驳异端》(Adv. Haer.)，IV. 8，§。↩︎

  21. Nonne et laici sacerdotes sumus?（我们平信徒不也是祭司吗？）↩︎

  22. 《劝守贞洁》(De Exhort. Cast.)，第7章。另参《论一夫一妻制》(De Monog.)，7、12；《论洗礼》(De Bapt.)，17；《论祷告》(De Orat.)，18。↩︎

  23. 《致哥林多人书》(Ad Cor.)，44：Συνευδοκησάσης* τῆς* ἐκκλησία*" πάσης*，consentiente universa ecclesia（全教会一致同意）。↩︎

  24. 《书信》(Ep.)，LX. 3–4（戈尔德霍恩 [Goldhorn] 版）。↩︎

  25. 《书信》(Ep.)，lv. 7：“Factus est Cornelius episcopus de Dei et Christi ejus judicio, de clericorum paene omnium testimonio, de plebis quae tum adfuit suffragio, et de sacerdotum antiquorum et bonorum virorum collegio.”（哥尼流成为主教，是出于上帝及其基督的裁断，凭着几乎全体教士的见证、当时在场会众的投票赞同，以及资深祭司和善良之人的集体认可。）↩︎

  26. Sine consensu plebis（未经会众同意）。↩︎

  27. 优西比乌 (Eusebius)，《教会史》(H. E.)，VI. 19：“在那里［凯撒利亚］，他［俄利根 (Origen)］虽尚未借按手领受祭司职分，主教们仍请他在教会中公开讲解圣经。”诚然，亚历山大主教底米丢 (Demetrius) 曾以此作为控告他的根据；但所控告的是俄利根“在主教面前”讲道，而不是他以平信徒身份讲道。耶路撒冷和凯撒利亚的主教还举出数例，说明圣洁的主教曾邀请有才干的平信徒向民众讲道。普鲁登提乌斯 (Prudentius) 和埃德修斯 (Aedesius) 尚为平信徒时，便在阿比西尼亚 (Abyssinia) 建立了教会；见苏格拉底 (Socrates)，《教会史》(Hist. Eccl.)，I. 19。↩︎

  28. 《使徒宪章》(Const. Apost.)，VIII. 31。伪安波罗修 (Ambrosiaster)，或称执事希拉里 (Hilary the Deacon)，在其《以弗所书注释》(Com. Ad Eph.) 4:11、12中说，早期“omnes docebant et omnes baptizabant”（人人都施教，人人都施洗）。↩︎

  29. 第98条教规：“Laicus praesentibus clericis nisi ipsis jubentibus, docere non audeat.”（有教士在场时，平信徒若未奉他们的吩咐，不得擅自施教。）第99条教规禁止妇女，无论多么“博学或圣洁”，都不得“擅自在聚会中教导男人”。教皇利奥一世 (Leo I.) 为维护教会秩序，禁止平信徒讲道（《书信》[Ep] 92、93）。查理大帝 (Charlemagne) 颁布法令规定：“平信徒不应在教会中诵读经课，也不应唱哈利路亚，只可唱不带哈利路亚的诗篇或应答句。”↩︎

  30. 希腊文本（此前仅知其一个残篇）由布林尼奥斯 (Bryennios) 于1875年发现并刊布，叙利亚文译本则由本斯利 (Bensley) 于1876年刊布。见哈纳克 (Harnack) 编《使徒教父集》(Patres Apost.)，第I卷；以及莱特福特 (Lightfoot)，《罗马的圣革利免》(S. Clement of Rome)，《附录》(Appendix)（1877年）。哈纳克、希尔根费尔德 (Hilgenfeld) 和哈奇 (Hatch)（前引书，第114页，注）认为这篇讲章是平信徒所讲；但莱特福特（第304页）认为，上文提及的措辞是一种常见的修辞手法，讲者借此将自己置于与听众相同的地位。↩︎

  31. Ὑποδιάκονοι*，subdiaconi（副执事），或许与《新约》和较早期教父著作中的 ὑπηρέται* 相同。↩︎

  32. Ἀναγνωσταί*，lectores（读经员），德尔图良 (Tertullian) 曾提及。↩︎

  33. Ἀκόλουθοι*，acolythi（辅祭员）。↩︎

  34. Ἐξορκισταί*，exorcistae（赶鬼者）。↩︎

  35. Δαιμονιζόμενοι*, ἐνεργούμενοι*↩︎

  36. Ψάλται*，psalmistae cantores（唱诗者）。↩︎

  37. θυρωροί*，πυλωροί*，ostiarii janitores（守门人）。↩︎

  38. 见优西比乌 (Eusebius)，vi. 43。↩︎

  39. 瑞典和丹麦的路德宗、美国的卫理宗以及摩拉维亚弟兄会 (Moravian) 的主教制便属此类，它们承认非主教制教会圣职的有效性。圣公会内部兼容高教会派和低教会派两种主教制理论，一种源自中世纪的圣统制，另一种源自宗教改革；但圣公会拒斥教皇首位权，视其为敌基督的僭越，尽管必须承认，这一制度几乎与主教制同样古老，其根源可以追溯至罗马的革利免 (Clement of Rome)，至少也可追溯至爱任纽 (Irenaeus) 的时代。↩︎

  40. 参优西比乌 (Eusebius)，vii. 27–30。↩︎

  41. 见吉泽勒 (Gieseler) 所引各段，第I卷，第282页及下页（纽约哈珀版）。↩︎

  42. 这是希腊教会、罗马天主教和圣公会高教会派的理论。欧洲大陆极少数新教人士，如本生爵士 (Chevalier Bunsen)、罗特 (Rothe) 和蒂尔施 (Thiersch，一位欧文派信徒)，也倡导此说，认为主教制源自以弗所的约翰。↩︎

  43. 路德宗、长老宗以及一些杰出的圣公会作家持此说。我们可以举出莫斯海姆 (Mosheim)、尼安德 (Neander)、莱特福特 (Lightfoot)、斯坦利 (Stanley)、哈奇 (Hatch)。还有纯以批评家身份作判断的鲍尔 (Baur) 和勒南 (Renan)。↩︎

  53. 《教会史》(H. E.)，III. 11。参IV. 22所载赫格西普 (Hegesippus) 的残篇。莱特福特在《腓立比书》(Philippians) 第202页针对罗特的推论指出：“赫格西普的记载把这次集会的目的仅限于任命圣雅各的继任者。倘若这次会议的商议果真如罗特的理论所假定的，对教会的未来产生了如此巨大而持久的影响，这位早期史家几乎不可能不知此事，或虽知此事却默而不言。”↩︎

  54. 《致哥林多人书》(Ad Corinth.)，第44章：Οἱ ἀπόστολοι ἡμῶν ἔγνωσαν διὰ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ὅτι ἔρις ἔσται ἐπὶ τοῦ ὀνόματος τῆς ἐπισκοπῆς. Διὰ ταύτην οὖν τὴν αἰτίαν πρόγνωσιν εἰληφότες τελείαν κατέστησαν τοὺς προειρημένους καὶ μεταξὺ ἐπινομὴν [or ἐπιμονὴν] ἔδωκαν, ὅπως, ἐὰν κοιμηθῶσιν, διαδέξωνται ἕτεροι δεδοκιμασμένοι ἄνδρες τὴν λειτουργίαν αὐτῶν.*。“我们的使徒借着我们的主耶稣基督，知道将来必有人为监督职分的名号起争执［即一般而言的教牧职分；参《使徒行传》1:20；七十士译本《民数记》4:16；《诗篇》109:8；《历代志下》23:18］。因此，他们既有完全的预知，就任命了前述诸人［即长老—监督和执事；参第42、57章］，随后又作了安排［若随莱特福特读作 ἐπιμονήν*，则意为作了延续的安排］，使这些人若睡了，便由其他经过认可的人接续他们的职事。”↩︎

  55. 此处读法晦涩，颇有争议。亚历山大抄本作 ἐπινομήν*，君士坦丁堡抄本作 ἐπιδομήν*（两者均有 EIII-OMHN）。前一词罕见（原文作“from or from νέμω* or fromνόμος*”，即涉及词源的说明），词典中未见收录；因此，学者提出了种种校改，如 ἀπονομήν*（尤尼乌斯 [Junius]）、ἐπιδοχήν*（布林尼奥斯 [Bryennios]）、ἐπιβολήν*（冯·格布哈特 [von Gebhardt] 与哈纳克 [Harnack]）、ἐπιμονήν*（本生 [Bunsen]、莱特福特 [Lightfoot]）、ἐπιτροπήν*（希尔根费尔德 [Hilgenfeld]）、ἐπιλογήν*、ἐπινομίαν*、ἐπιστολήν*、ἐπιταγήν*、ἔτι νόμον*。罗特 (Rothe)（《起源》[Anfänge]，第374页）巧妙地将 ἐπινομήν* 译为“遗嘱安排”（testamentarische Verfügung = ἐπινομίς*，一项追加法令、遗嘱附录），并将其等同于爱任纽残篇中的 δεύτεραι διατάξεις*。但最新的编者认为此说不能成立，予以否定。莱特福特（随本生）读作 ἐπιμονήν*，意为持续（并非本生所译的“终身任职”）。不过，这段话的旨意，与其说取决于这个词的含义，不如说取决于使徒还是会众的职员，才是后面动词 κοιμηθῶσιν* 在语法上的主语。↩︎

  56. 另见格布哈特 (Gebhardt) 与哈纳克 (Harnack)（presbyteri et diaconi illi, quos apostoli ipsi constituerunt，即“使徒亲自设立的那些长老和执事”）、罗马天主教编者丰克 (Funk)（“κοιμηθῶσιν*，sc. episcopi et diaconi de quorum successione Clemens agit”，即“所指为革利免论及其继任问题的监督和执事”），以及莱特福特主教（“使徒亲自任命的第一代长老”）。关于这整段文字，另参莱特福特，《腓立比书》(Philippians)，第203页，其中驳斥了罗特的解释；鲍尔 (Baur)，《主教制的起源》(Ursprung des Episcopats)，第53页；埃瓦尔德 (Ewald)，《以色列民族史》(Gesch. des Volkes Israel)，VII. 300；里奇尔 (Ritschl)，《古代大公教会》(Altkath. K.)，第358、413页；以及伊尔根费尔德 (Ilgenfeld)，《使徒教父》(Apost. Väter)，第70页。↩︎

  57. 因此，罗特将这一制度追溯至约翰。莱特福特主教（《腓立比书》[Philippians]，第204页）也倾向于此说：“小亚细亚 (Asia Minor) 即使不是外邦教会主教制的母亲，也是它的乳母。在一个以圣约翰为活的中心和引导者的基督徒群体中，如此重要的一项制度，若无他的认可，几乎不可能发展起来；而且早期传统十分明确地将他的名字与这些地区主教的任命联系在一起。”他在《伊格那修与坡旅甲》(Ignat. and Polyc.)，I. 377中，更为肯定地重申了这一见解。↩︎

  58. 《使徒行传》20:17、28；《腓立比书》1:1；《提多书》1:5；《提摩太前书》3:1–7、8–13；《彼得前书》5:1、2。参作者《使徒教会史》(Hist. of the Apost. Ch.)，§§132、133，第522–531页（纽约版）；以及第I卷，第492页及后。↩︎

  59. 第42章。参莱特福特的注释：“他不可能略去长老，尤其因为他的唯一目的，正是维护他们遭到攻击的权柄。因此，ἐπίσκοπος* 和 πρεσβύτερος* 这两个词，在革利免那里与在使徒著作中一样，都是同义词。直到伊格那修 (Ignatius) 和坡旅甲 (Polycarp) 的著作中，它们才首次作为不同的头衔出现。”↩︎

  64. 《提多书注释》(Ad Titum)，i. 7。参《书信》(Epist.)，83、85。↩︎

  65. 《提多书注释》(Ad Tit.)，i. 7：“Sicut ergo presbyteri sciunt, see ex ecclesiae consuetudine ei, qui sibi praepositus fuerit, esse subjectos, ita episcopi noverint, se magis consuetudine quam dispositionis Dominicae veritate presbyteris esse majores et in commune debere ecclesiam regere.”（因此，正如长老知道，他们服从那被立在自己之上的人，是出于教会的惯例；主教也应知道，他们高于长老，与其说确实出于主的规定，不如说是出于惯例，并且他们应当共同治理教会。）罗马执事希拉里 (Hilary，即伪安波罗修 [Ambrosiaster]) 在《提摩太前书》3:10的注释中说：“Hic enim episcopus est, qui inter presbyteros primus est.”（因为在长老中居首的，就是主教。）另参金口约翰 (Chrysostom)，《提摩太前书讲道集》(Hom. in Epist. 1 ad Tim.)，xi. 38。↩︎

  66. 《致埃万格鲁书》(Epist. ad Evangelum)（《全集》[Opp.]，iv，第802页，马蒂内 [Martinay] 编）：Alexandriae a Marco evangelista usque ad Heraclam et Dionysium episcopos presbyteri semper unum ex se electum in excelsiori gradu collocatum episcopum nominabant, quomodo si exercitus imperatorem faciat, aut diaconi elegant de se, quem industrium noverint et archidiaconum vocent.（在亚历山大，从传福音的马可直到赫拉克拉 [Heraclas] 和狄奥尼修 [Dionysius] 两位主教的时代，长老总是从自己中间选出一人，将他置于较高的地位，称他为主教；这正如军队推举统帅，或执事从自己中间选出一位他们知道勤勉能干的人，称他为总执事。）↩︎

  67. 牛津版，1658年，第331页：“Constituit evangelista Marcus una cum Hakania patriarcha duodecim presbyteros, qui nempe cum patriarcha manerent, adeo ut cum vacaret patriachatus, unum e duodecim presbyteris eligerent, cnius capiti reliqui undecim manus imponentes ipsi benedicerent et patriarcham crearent, deinde virum aliquem insignem eligerent, quem secum presbyterum constituerent,loco ejus, qui factus est patriarcha, ut ita semper exstarent duodecim. Neque desiit Alexandriae institutum hoc de presbyteris, ut scilcet patriarchas crearent ex presbyteris duodecim, usque ad tempera Alexandri patriarchae Alexandriae. Is autem vetuit, ne deinceps patriarcham presbyteri crearent. Et decrervit, ut mortuo patriarcha convenient episcopi, qui patriarcham ordinarent.”（传福音的马可与牧首哈卡尼亚 [Hakania] 一同设立了十二位长老，使他们与牧首同在；牧首之位出缺时，他们便从十二位长老中选出一位，其余十一位按手在他头上，为他祝福，立他为牧首；然后再选一位杰出的人，立为与他们同列的长老，接替那位已成为牧首的人，使长老始终保持十二位。在亚历山大，从十二位长老中选立牧首的这一长老制度，一直延续到亚历山大牧首亚历山大 [Alexander] 的时代。他禁止长老此后再选立牧首，并规定牧首去世后，由主教们集会，按立牧首。）↩︎

  69. 哈奇 (Hatch) 在其班普顿讲座《早期基督教会的组织》(The Organization of the Early Christian Churches) 第II讲中，十分充分地阐明了主教制在慈善与财务方面的性质。↩︎

  70. 真伪问题将在§165讨论。丘尔顿 (Cureton，1845年)、本生 (Bunsen)、利普修斯 (Lipsius) 等人接受叙利亚文译本为伊格那修书信的原始形式，认为连希腊文短本也遭到了窜改，但仍将其年代定于第二世纪中叶。罗特 (Rothe)、黑费勒 (Hefele)、沙弗（初版）、迪斯特迪克 (Düsterdieck)、乌尔霍恩 (Uhlhorn)、察恩 (Zahn)、哈纳克 (Harnack) 则维护希腊文较短修订本的真实性。希腊文长本已被一致认定为伪作而放弃。圣统制的起源因虔诚的欺诈而变得晦暗不明。见下文§164、165。↩︎

  71. 叙利亚文《致坡旅甲书》(Polycarp) 中，“主教”一词出现四次；叙利亚文《致以弗所人书》(Ephesians) 则称颂上帝，因他赐给他们阿尼西母 (Onesimus) 这样一位主教。希腊文较短本《致坡旅甲书》在问安中提及主教职分，在全书八章中的三章也有提及（第5章两次，第6章两次，第8章一次）。希腊文《致以弗所人书》共21章，其中“主教”一词出现十三次，“长老”三次，“执事”一次（在前六章和第21章中）。希腊文《致特拉勒人书》(Trallians) 中，主教出现九次；《致马格尼西亚人书》(Magnesians) 中十一次；《致非拉铁非人书》(Philadelphians) 中八次；《致士每拿人书》(Smynaeans) 中九次。因此，三封叙利亚文书信仅六次提到主教；七封希腊文较短本书信则约有五十次；但措辞最强烈的段落之一，却见于叙利亚文《致坡旅甲书》（第5、6章）。↩︎

  72. 唯一的例外是，伊格那修自称“叙利亚的主教”，说自己“蒙了上帝的恩惠，从东方被送往西方”（第2章）。其中也使用了动词 ἐπισκοπέω*，但所指的是基督（第9章）。↩︎

  74. 察恩 (Zahn) 在《致坡旅甲书》(Ad Polyc.) 第5章采用的读法是：ἐὰν γνωσθῇ πλέον τοῦ ἐπισκόπου*，即他比主教更为人所知，或更受尊重。另一读法是 πλήν*，意为越过，或离开。↩︎

  84. 比较《驳异端》(Adv. Haer.) III. 3，§1、2；4，1；IV. 33，§8。我记得，1844年我在牛津 (Oxford) 见到已故的波西博士 (Dr. Posey) 时，他极其看重爱任纽 (Irenaeus) 为主教职分不间断承继这一教义所作的见证，视其为真正大公教会不可或缺的标志；但他却忽略了同时发展的首席权，而一年以后，正是这首席权将他的朋友 J. H. 纽曼 (J. H. Newman) 带入了罗马教会。在一切信仰和教会纪律的事务上，新约是唯一可靠的指南和最终准则。莱特福特 (Lightfoot) 对爱任纽有关主教制的教导作了很好的阐述（前引书，第237页）：“爱任纽继伊格那修 (Ignatius) 而起，其间相隔约两代。随着教会处境的改变，主教职分的面貌也发生了变化。此时的宗教空气中，充斥着各式各样的异端思辨。彼此对立的教师竞相争取支持，困惑的信徒便要求一种决定性的标准，来检验争论各方的主张。爱任纽对这个问题作出了回答。他论证说：‘若要确知使徒的教义，就当求诸使徒的教会。’主教的承继可以追溯至最初的时代，他们由使徒亲自设立；在这承继中，纯正信仰的传递便有了保证，而这是任何孤立的、新近兴起的、自立的教师所不能提供的。例如罗马教会，其主教谱系的每一环都完备无缺，而其最早的主教利努斯 (Linus) 和革利免 (Clement)，曾与使徒本人交往；又如士每拿 (Smyrna) 教会，其主教坡旅甲 (Polycarp) 是圣约翰的门徒，去世还没有多久。因此，这时主教职分与其说被视为教会合一的中心，不如说被视为使徒传统的保管者。”↩︎

  87. Non ecclesia numerus episcoporum（主教的数目并不构成教会）。《论贞洁》(De Pudic.) 第21章。比较§42，第128页。↩︎

  88. “正如居普良 (Cyprian) 完成了主教权力的大厦，他也是第一个毫不缓和、毫不掩饰地提出祭司权利要求的人；他坚持这些要求的语气如此不容妥协，以至他的继承人除了贯彻他的原则、重申他的措辞以外，已无事可做。”莱特福特 (Lightfoot)，前引书，第257页。“若说在伊格那修看来，主教是基督徒合一的中心，在爱任纽看来，他是使徒传统的保管者，那么在居普良看来，他就是属灵事务上基督的全权代表。”同上，第238页。↩︎

  89. 《书信集》(Epist.) lxvi. 3。比较书信 lv. 20：Christianus non est, qui in Christi ecclesia non est（不在基督教会之内的，就不是基督徒）。↩︎

  90. 《论教会的合一》(De Unit. Eccl.) 第5章：Episcopatus unus est, cujus a singulis in solidum pars tenetur.（主教职分是一个，每位主教都共同持有其中一分，而对整体负责。）比较《书信集》(Ep.) lv. 20：Quum sit a Christo una ecclesia per totum mundum in multa membra divisa, item episcopatus unus episcoporum multorum concordi numerositate diffusus（既然由基督而来的教会只有一个，遍布全世界而分为许多肢体，主教职分也同样只有一个，分布于众多和睦同心的主教之中）。↩︎

  91. 第3条教规：Presbyter quum ordinatur, episcopo eum benedicente et manum super caput ejus tenente, etiam omnes presbyteri, qui praesentes sunt, manus suas juxta manum episcopi super caput illius teneant.（按立长老时，主教为他祝福，并将手放在他头上；所有在场的长老，也当将手放在他的头上，紧挨着主教的手。）↩︎

  92. 《亚历山大宗主教欧提基乌斯编年史》，波科克译本 (Eutychii Patriarchae Alexandr. Annal. interpr. Pocockio)（牛津〔Oxon.〕，1658年，I，第331页）。见第141页所引段落。↩︎

  93. 或称伪安布罗斯 (Ambrosiaster)，《以弗所书注释》(Ad Eph.) iv. 11。↩︎

  94. 《讲道集》(Hom.) iii. 60、62、66、70。《克莱门致雅各书》(Ep. Clem. ad Jac.) 17。比较《辨认集》(Recogn.) iii. 66。↩︎

  95. 《讲道集》(Hom.) xi. 36；《辨认集》(Recogn.) iii. 66；vi. 15。↩︎

  96. Ἐπίσκοπος ἐπισκόπων*，《讲道集》(Hom.) xi. 35；《辨认集》(Recogn.) iv. 35。↩︎

  97. 乡村主教（χωρεπίσκοποι*）最早见于314年的安居拉 (Ancyra) 和新凯撒利亚 (Neo-Caesarea) 会议，后来又见于尼西亚 (Nicaea) 会议。他们在东方一直存在到第9世纪，随后由督察（ἔξαρχοι*）取代。在西方，乡村主教（chorepiscopi）履行通常的主教职能，却没有适当地隶属于教区主教，因此激起了嫉妒和敌意，直到查理大帝 (Charlemagne) 时期这一职分被废除，此后仅作为各类座堂高级教士的头衔而存留。见哈丹 (Haddan) 撰文，载史密斯与奇瑟姆 (Smith & Cheetham)《基督教古事辞典》(Dict. Chr. Ant.) I. 354，以及该处所引文献。↩︎

  98. μητροπόλεις*。μητροπολῖται* 一词由此而来。↩︎

  99. Sedes apostolicae, matrices ecclesiae.（使徒教座，母教会。）↩︎

  100. Ἡ* ἐκκλησία* τοῦ θεοῦ, ἡ παροικοῦσα Ῥώμην τῇ* ἐκκλησίᾳ*/ τοῦ θεοῦ, τῇ παροικούσῃ Κόρινθον.*。“寄居罗马的上帝的教会，致在哥林多哀恸的上帝的教会！”Πάροικος* 指暂居者，κάτοικος* 指永久居民。这里的基督徒显为世上的客旅和寄居者，他们的家在天上；比较《彼得前书》1:17；2:11；《希伯来书》11:13。↩︎

  101. 从新近发现的接近篇末的部分，即第59、62、63章（布里恩尼奥斯〔Bryennios〕版，君士坦丁堡〔Const.〕，1875年），这一点看得十分清楚。这几章为教皇统治的起源提供了新的光照。比较莱特福特 (Lightfoot) 在《罗马的圣革利免补编》(Appendix to S. Clement of Rome)（伦敦〔Lond.〕，1877年）第252页及后所作的审慎评论。↩︎

  102. 从这封书信本身可以十分清楚地看出，当时罗马会众仍由一个长老团治理（正如朗根〔Langen〕在前引书第81页所说，collegialisch, nicht monarchisch，即合议制，而非君主制）。↩︎

  103. Προκαθημένη τῆς ἀγάπης*，praesidens in caritate（在爱中居首位）。见题署。察恩 (Zahn) 在其版本第75页说：“In caritatis operibus semper primum locum sibi vindicavit ecclesia Romana.”（罗马教会在爱心的工作上，始终居于首位。）有些罗马天主教作者（如默勒〔Möhler〕，《教父学》〔Patrol.〕I. 144）以十分牵强的圣统制意义，将这句话解释为：“基督教世界之爱的联合体的首领”（Vorsteherin des Liebesbundes）。Agape 从不意指教会，而是指爱，或爱筵。见朗根，前引书，第94页。↩︎

  104. 优西比乌 (Euseb.)，《教会史》(Hist. Eccl.) IV. 23，10：ἐξ ἀρχῆς ὑμῖν ἔθος ἐστὶ τοῦτο, πάντας μὲν ἀδελφοὺς ποικίλως εὐεργετεῖν, ἐκκλησίαις τε πολλαῖς ταῖς κατὰ πᾶσαν πόλιν ἐφόδια πέμπειν*↩︎

  105. 《驳异端》(Adv. Haer.) iii. §2中这段著名的话，仅存拉丁文本，其解释颇有争议：“Ad hanc enim ecclesiam propter potentiorem（据马叙埃〔Massuet〕的推定：potiorem）principalitatem necesse est omnem convenire ecclesia, hoc est, eos qui sunt undique fideles, in qua semper ab his, qui sunt undique, conservata est ab apostolis traditio.”（因为这教会具有更强的首要地位，所有教会，即各地的信徒，都必须与它一致；在这教会中，来自使徒的传统始终由各地的人保存着。）希腊原文可能是：Πρὸς ταύτην γὰρ τὴν ἐκκλησίαν διὰ τὴν ἱκανωτέραν πρωτεῖαν συμβαίνειν*（或就地方意义而言，συνέρχεσθαι*）δεῖ*（另有人认为是 ἀνάγκη*，意为自然的必然性）πᾶσαν τὴν ἐκκλησίαν*，等等。重点在于 principalitas，它很可能对应 πρωτεία*（蒂尔施〔Thiersch〕和吉泽勒〔Gieseler〕持此看法）。比较爱任纽 IV. 38，3，其中 πρωτεύει* 被译作 principatitatem habet（具有首要地位）。然而，施蒂伦 (Stieren) 和齐格勒 (Ziegler)（《爱任纽》〔Irenaeus〕，1871年，第152页）将 propter potentiorem principalitatem 译为：διὰ τὴν ἱκανωτέραν ἀρχαιότητα*，“由于更为久远的历史”。关于整段文字，比较蒂尔施发表于《研究与评论》(Studien und Kritiken) 1842年、第512页及后的论文；吉泽勒 I. 1，第214页（§51）；施内曼 (Schneemann)：《圣爱任纽关于罗马教会首位权之见证的评注与辩护》(Sancti Irenaei de ecclesia Romanae principatu testimonium commentatum et defensum)，弗赖堡 (Freiburg i. B.)，1870年；以及朗根 (Langen)，前引书，第170页及后。朗根（属德林格〔Döllinger〕学派的旧天主教徒）解释说：“Die potior principalitas bezeichnet den Vorrang, welchen die Kirche der Hauptptstadt als solche vor alten übrigen Kirchen besass ... die Hauptstadt war das Centrum des damaligen Weltverkehrs, und in Folge dessen der Sammelplats von Christen aller Art.”（“这 potior principalitas 指首都教会因其身处首都而在其他一切教会之前所享有的优先地位……首都是当时世界交往的中心，因此也是各类基督徒的汇集之地。”）他援引波尔多的赫尔韦乌斯 (Herveus of Bordeaux) 和胡戈·埃特里阿努斯 (Hugo Eterianus) 的平行段落，来为 convenire 的地方性含义辩护（第172页及下页）。但其道德意义（意见一致）似乎更为自然。↩︎

  106. Petri cathedram atque ecclesiam principalem, unde unitas sacerdotalis exorta est.（彼得的教座和首要教会，祭司职分的合一由此而出。）《书信集》(Epist.) lv，第19章（Bal.版），《致罗马主教哥尼流》(Ad Cornelium episc. Rom.)。在戈尔德霍恩 (Goldhorn) 版中为书信 lix. 19。↩︎

  107. Ecclesiae catholicae radicem et matricem（大公教会的根与母体）。书信 xl. 2，Bal.版（Goldh.版为 xlviii）。居普良 (Cyprian) 著作中其他有利于罗马教座的段落，不是插入之文，就是为教皇制利益而作的篡改。↩︎

  108. 爱任纽 (Irenaeus) 逐一列举了从革利免到埃留特鲁斯 (Eleutherus，177年) 的罗马主教，却只承认其中一位是殉道者，即特勒斯福鲁斯 (Telesphorus)；他说此人：ὃς* καὶ ἐνδόξως ἐμαρτύρησε*，P，《驳异端》(Adv. Haer.) III，第3章，§3。优西比乌 (Eusebius)《教会史》(H. E.) V. 6也如此记载。我们必须据此判断罗马天主教在这一点上传统的价值。它距离所论时代如此遥远，完全不值得相信。↩︎

  109. 纽曼枢机 (Cardinal Newman) 说（《辩白》〔Apologia〕，第407页）：“在整个逼迫时期，罗马教座没有一位伟大的思想家。此后很长一段时间，它也拿不出一位教会博士。西方世界伟大的明灯是圣奥古斯丁；他虽不是无谬误的教师，却塑造了欧洲的思想。”斯坦利座堂主任牧师 (Dean Stanley) 评论说（《基督教制度》〔Christian Institutions〕，第241页）：“君士坦丁堡、亚历山大和坎特伯雷的教座上，曾有一些任职者，他们的言论对基督教世界思想的影响，超过了任何一位教皇。”↩︎

  110. 他在《驳斥一切异端》(Philosophumenon) 第九卷中称他为 ἀνὴρ ἰδιώτης καὶ αἰσχροκέρδης*。↩︎

  111. 或至少是第一位由彼得任命的。德尔图良 (Tertullian)，《论异端者的抗辩》(De Praescr. HaeR.) 第32章：“Romanorum Clementem a Petro ordinatum.”（罗马人的革利免由彼得按立。）《使徒宪章》(Apost. Const.) VII. 6以利努斯 (Linus，比较《提摩太后书》4:21) 为首任主教，由保罗任命；革利免为第二任，由彼得任命。按埃皮法尼乌 (Epiphanius) 的说法（《驳异端》〔Haer.〕XXVII. 6），革利免由彼得按立，却直到利努斯和阿纳克勒图斯 (Anacletus) 去世后才就职。↩︎

  112. 爱任纽 (Irenaeus) 的名录（《驳异端》〔Adv. Haer.〕III. 3，3）一直列到他自己的时代（公元177年），如下：使徒彼得和保罗，利努斯 (Linos)，阿纳克勒图斯 (Anacletos)，革利免 (Clement)，埃瓦里斯图斯 (Evaristus)，亚历山大 (Alexander)，克叙斯图斯 (Xystos)，光荣殉道的特勒斯福鲁斯 (Telesphoros)，许吉努斯 (Hyginos)，庇乌斯 (Pios)，阿尼凯图斯 (Aniketos)，索特尔 (Soter)，埃留特鲁斯 (Eleutheros)；后者当时“从使徒算起，在第十二位上承受了主教职分”。爱任纽又说：“按着这一次序，借着这一承继，来自使徒的教会传统和真理的宣讲，传到了我们这里。”↩︎

  113. 朗根 (Langen)（前引书，第100页及后）将罗马长老—主教的世系一直延续到亚历山大，并将罗马教会君主制体制（即教区主教制）的起始，定于图拉真 (Trajan) 或哈德良 (Hadrian) 时代。爱任纽（见优西比乌 V. 27）将直到阿尼凯图斯 (Anicetus，154年) 为止的罗马主教称为 πρεσβύτεροι*。↩︎

  114. 英格兰教会 (Church of England) 在信经中保留了“大公”一词（catholic），也保留了这一词在教皇制以前及反对教皇制时的用法（即一般的、普世的）；而路德 (Luther) 在其《教理问答》中，摩拉维亚教会 (Moravian church) 在其礼仪中，都以“基督教的”（Christian）取代此词，将“catholic”的使用权让给了罗马天主教徒。“罗马”是一个宗派性用语（与希腊大公教会和福音派大公教会相对）。↩︎

  115. Credo ecclesiam（我信教会）；但不像论及神的各位格时那样，使用 in（εἰς*）ecclesiam（信靠教会）。↩︎

  116. Communio sanctorum（圣徒相通）。不过，第五世纪以前罗马教会的原始信经中，并没有这一条。↩︎

  117. ἄνθρωπον εἰς ἕνωσιν κατηρτισμένον*。↩︎

  118. 《致士每拿人书》(Ad Smyrn.) 第8章。↩︎

  119. 《致以弗所人书》(Ad Ephes.) 第5章。《致特拉勒人书》(Ad Trall.) 第7章。《致非拉铁非人书》(Ad Philad.) 第3章，等等。↩︎

  120. 《驳异端》(Adv. Haer.) iii. 24。“Ubi ecclesia ibi et Spiritus Dei, et ubi Spiritus Dei, illic et omnis gratia”（教会在哪里，上帝的灵也在哪里；上帝的灵在哪里，一切恩典也都在那里）。新教则会反过来说，将圣灵放在首位：“Ubi Spiritus Dei, ibi ecclesia et omnis gratia.”（上帝的灵在哪里，教会和一切恩典也都在那里。）↩︎

  121. 《约书亚记讲道》(Hom. 3 in Josuam)，第5章。“Extra hanc domum, id est extra ecclesiam, nemo salvatur”（在这家之外，也就是在教会之外，无人得救）。↩︎

  122. “Christianus non est, qui in Christi ecclesia non est”（不在基督教会之内的人，就不是基督徒）。↩︎

  123. “Habere non potest Deum patrem, qui ecclesiam non habet matrem”（不以教会为母的人，就不能以上帝为父）。↩︎

  124. “Extra ecclesia nulla salus”（教会之外没有救恩）。然而，他从未说过“extra Romanam nulla salus”（罗马教会之外没有救恩）。↩︎

  125. 《约翰福音》10:16。拉丁文《武加大译本》(Vulgate) 将 ποίμνη* 译作 ovile（羊圈），把它与 αὐλή* 混淆；我们可以说，这是一个颇具其特色、也预示不祥的错误。《钦定本》(Authorized Version) 沿袭了这个贻害不浅的误译（“一个羊圈”），但1881年的《修订本》(Revision) 已予以纠正。↩︎

  126. 哈奇 (Hatch)，前引书（l.c.），第187页及下页。↩︎

  127. Concilium（会议）一词，最早由德尔图良 (Tertullian) 用于教会意义，见《论禁食》(De Iejun.) 第13章、《论贞洁》(De Pudic.) 第10章；σύνοδος*，意为集会、议事会议（见希罗多德、修昔底德、柏拉图、德摩斯梯尼等人的著作），最早用于基督徒集会，见伪托使徒的《宪章》(Constit.) V. 20，以及《教规》(Canons) 第36或38条。它可以指教区、省区或全体基督徒为选举、司法、立法或教义事务而召开的会议。↩︎

  128. 公元50年。《使徒行传》15章和《加拉太书》2章。另参主的应许：即使只有最少数的人奉他的名聚集，他也必在他们中间，见《马太福音》18:19、20。参本书第I卷，§64，第503页及后。↩︎

  129. 关于罗马帝国的行省会议，参马夸特 (Marquardt)《罗马国家行政》(Römische Staatsverwaltung)，I. 365–377；以及哈奇 (Hatch)，前引书（l.c.），第164页及后。议事之前先行献祭，会议主席称为大祭司。↩︎

  130. 也就是说，范围限于旧罗马帝国之内，将其视为 orbis terrarum（天下）。从来没有过绝对普世的会议。即使325至787年间的七次大公会议，也仅限于帝国境内，西方主教出席者寥寥。在此之后召开的罗马教会会议（直到1870年的梵蒂冈会议），虽声称具有普世性，却将希腊教会和一切福音派教会排除在外。↩︎

  131. 参《使徒行传》15:6、7、12、13、23，其中除使徒和长老外，还明确提到“弟兄们”也是会议成员，甚至参与最后的决议和牧函。关于文本异读，参本书第I卷，第505页。↩︎

  132. 居普良 (Cyprian)，《著作集》(Opera)，巴吕兹版（ed. Baluz.），第329页。不过，在这次会议的记录中（第330–338页），只有主教以投票者身份出现；有些作者由此推断，平信徒乃至长老都没有 votum decisium（决定性表决权）。但在若干古代会议中，长老和执事曾在主教之后署名；参阿尔杜安 (Harduin)，《会议汇编》(Coll. Conc.)，I. 250、266；赫费勒 (Hefele)，I. 19。↩︎

  133. 《书信集》(Epp.) xi、xiii、lxvi、lxxi。↩︎

  134. 《书信》(Ep.) xxxi。↩︎

  135. 居普良 (Cyprian)，《书信》(Ep.) liv，其根据是《使徒行传》15:28的 ἔδοξε τῷ ἁγίῳ πνεύματι καὶ ἡμῖν*，visum est Spiritui Sancto et nobis（圣灵和我们定意如此）。公元314年的阿尔勒会议 (Council of Arles) 也如此说：Placuit ergo, presente Spiritu Sancto et angelis ejus（因此，在圣灵及其天使临在之下，议决如下）；见阿尔杜安 (Harduin)，《会议汇编》(Coll. Concil.)，I. 262。↩︎

  136. Epistolae formatae，γράμματα τετυπωμένα*（格式书信）。↩︎

  137. 这一政策由君士坦丁一世 (Constantine I) 于公元326年开创（《狄奥多西法典》〔Cod. Theod.〕16, 5, 1）。他在后来的法令中，将313年曾赐予基督徒的特权和豁免，仅限于“Catholicae legis observatoribus”（遵守大公教会律法者）。他批准尼西亚信经，并放逐阿里乌 (Arius，325年)，尽管后来态度又有摇摆，并由一位半阿里乌派主教为他施洗（337年）。他的直接继承者也同样摇摆不定。但一般而言，拜占庭皇帝都承认会议在教义和纪律方面的决议，阻挠并最终禁止持异议者组织宗派。国家当然无法阻止作为个人见解的异议；它只能禁止并惩罚公开的宣认。充分的宗教自由要求政教分离。↩︎

  138. 赫费勒 (Hefele)、加姆斯 (Gams) 和戴尔 (Dale) 均赞同这一年代，而不采纳题记所标明的尼西亚会议时期（324年）。主要理由是：科尔多瓦主教何西乌 (Hosius of Cordova) 在324年身处东方，不可能出席；307年以后也不可能出席，因为那年他已加入君士坦丁的随从，成为其私人顾问之一。↩︎

  139. “Placuit picturas in ecclesia esse non debere, ne quod colitur et adoratur in parietibus depingatur.”“议决教会内不应有画像，免得所尊崇的〔圣徒〕和所敬拜的〔上帝与基督〕被描绘在墙上。”↩︎

  140. 后一种解释，是德罗西 (DeRossi) 在《地下罗马》(Roma sotteranea)，第I卷，第97页，以及赫费勒 (Hefele)，I. 170，对这条教规所作的解释。但戴尔 (Dale，第292页及后) 认为，它所针对的是基督徒的偶像崇拜。↩︎

  141. 关于埃尔维拉会议 (Synod of Elvira)，最好的论述见斐迪南·德·门多萨 (Ferdinand de Mendoza)，《致克雷芒八世论确认埃尔维拉会议》(De confirmando Concilio Illiberitano ad Clementem VIII.)，1593年（重印于曼西〔Mansi〕II. 57–397）；Fr. Ant. 冈萨雷斯 (Fr. Ant. Gonzalez)，《西班牙教会教规汇编》(Collect. Can. Ecclesiae Hispaniae)，马德里，1808年，附西班牙语译文的新版，1849年（重印于布伦斯〔Bruns〕，Bibl. Eccl.，第I卷，第II部，第1页及后）；赫费勒 (Hefele)，《会议史》(Conciliengesch.)，I. 148–192（第2版，1873年；或第1版，第122页及后）；加姆斯 (Gams)，《西班牙教会史》(Kirchengesch. von Spanien)，1864年，第II卷，第1–136页；以及戴尔 (Dale) 关于《埃尔维拉会议》(Synod of Elvira) 的专著，伦敦，1882年。↩︎

  142. Concilium Arelatense（阿尔勒会议），得名于 Arelate 或 Arelatum Sextanorum，即高卢东南部的罗马主要城市之一阿尔勒 (Arles)。君士坦丁一度居住于此，后来西哥特王欧里克 (Eurich) 也曾居住于此。这里或许是高卢第一个主教区的所在地，或者仅次于里昂 (Lyons) 和维埃纳 (Vienne)。该城举行过数次会议，第二次是在阿里乌派争论期间的353年。↩︎

  143. 不是麦克林托克与斯特朗 (McClintock & Strong)《百科全书》(Cyclop)“阿尔勒”(Arles) 条目所说的633年。↩︎

  144. 参优西比乌 (Eus.)，《教会史》(H. E.) x. 5；曼西 (Mansi)，II. 463–468；明兴 (München)，《第一次阿尔勒会议》(Das ersten Concil von Arles)，载《波恩哲学与天主教神学杂志》(Bonner Zeitschrift für Philos. und kath. Theol.)，第9、26、27期；以及赫费勒 (Hefele)，I. 201–219（第2版）。↩︎

  145. 赫费勒 (Hefele)，第I卷，第222页及后，列出教规的希腊文及德文，并附解释。他称其为 Synodus plenaria，即小亚细亚和叙利亚各教会的全体会议。另参曼西 (Mansi)，II. 514及后。358和375年，安居拉 (Ancyra) 曾举行过两次阿里乌派会议。↩︎

  146. 参赫费勒 (Hefele)，I. 242–251。↩︎

  147. 哈纳克 (Harnack，前引书，第266–268页) 认为伪克莱门 (Pseudo-Clement) 与伪伊格那丢 (Pseudo-Ignatius) 是同一人，并将其年代定于第四世纪中叶。↩︎

  148. 图里亚努斯 (Turrianus)、博维乌斯 (Bovius)，以及性情古怪的惠斯顿 (Whiston)，都将这些伪托使徒的《宪章》视为使徒的真实著作，认为其中包含基督在复活与升天之间四十天内的教导。但巴罗尼乌斯 (Baronius)、贝拉明 (Bellarmin) 和佩塔维乌斯 (Petavius) 并不看重它们，而新教学者达耶 (Daillé) 和布隆代尔 (Blondel) 则抨击并推翻了其真实性与权威性。这部著作逐渐累积而成，包含许多重复、插文、矛盾以及时代错置。已遭斩首的雅各（公元44年），被安排与保罗一同参加会议（VI. 14），但在另一处却被说成已经去世（V. 7）。使徒们谴责使徒时期以后的异端及异端人物（VI. 8），并为自己的逝世指定纪念日（VIII. 33）。其中对主教制的颂扬极为夸张。P. 德·拉加德 (P. de Lagarde) 说（Rel juris Eccles. ant.，序言，第IV页）：“Communis vivorum doctorum fere omnium nunc invaluit opinio eas [constitutiones] saeculo tertio clam succrevisse et quum sex aliquando libris septimo et octavo auctas esse postea.”（如今，几乎所有在世学者都普遍认为，这些〔宪章〕是在第三世纪悄然形成的，原来一度只有六卷，后来又增补了第七和第八卷。）↩︎

  149. 比克尔 (Bickell) 即持此说。贝弗里奇 (Beveridge) 则将这部汇编定于第三世纪。↩︎

  150. 据达耶 (Daillé)、冯·德赖博士 (Dr. von Drey) 和迈耶尔 (Mejer) 的看法。↩︎

  151. Peccata mortalia，或 ad mortem（致死的罪）；这是根据对《约翰一书》5:16颇为武断的解释。德尔图良列举了七种致死的罪： Homocidium idololatria, fraus, negatio blasphemia. utique et moechia et. fornicatio et si qua alia violatio templi Dei.（杀人、拜偶像、欺诈、背道、亵渎，当然还有奸淫、淫乱，以及任何其他玷污上帝之殿的行为。）见《论贞洁》(De pudic.) 第19章。他宣称这些罪是 irremissibilia（不可赦免的），horum ultra exoratur non erit Christus（基督不会再为这些罪受恳求）；也就是说，若这些罪是在受洗之后犯下的；因为洗礼洗净先前的一切罪责。因此，他劝人推迟受洗。↩︎

  152. Peccata, venialia（可赦免的罪）。↩︎

  153. Poenitentes（悔罪者）。↩︎

  154. 第4条教规及后。参赫费勒 (Hefele)，《会议史》(Conciliengesch)，第2版，I. 225及后。另参新凯撒利亚 (Neocaesarea) 第5条教规，以及赫费勒，第246页。↩︎

  155. Προσκλαίοντες*，flentes（哀哭者）；也称 χειμάζοντες*，hiemantes（在寒冬中露立者）。↩︎

  156. Ἀκροώμενοι*，audientes，或 auditores（听道者）。尼西亚会议第14条教规（赫费勒〔Hefele〕I. 418）规定：“跌倒的慕道者，应当在三年内仅作听道者，此后才可与慕道者一同祷告。”↩︎

  157. Γονυκλίνοντες*，genuflectentes（跪拜者）；又称 ὑποπίπτοντες*，Substrati（俯伏者）。γόνυ κλίνων* 一词用作一类悔罪者的名称，仅见于314年之后、325年之前召开的新凯撒利亚会议 (Council of Neocaesarea) 第5条教规。↩︎

  158. Συνιστάμενοι*，consistentes（同立者）。↩︎

  159. Πρόσκλαυσις*，fletus（哀哭）；ἀκρόασις*，auditus（听道）；ὑπόπτωσις*，prostratio, humiliatio（俯伏、谦卑）；σύστασις*，consistentia（同立）。有人认为，后三类对应于慕道者的三种类别，但并无充分根据。慕道者只有一类，至多也只有两类。参下文§72。↩︎

  160. Πρεσβύτεροι ἐπὶ τῆς μετανοίας*，presbyteri poenitentiarii（主管悔罪事务的长老）。↩︎

  161. Reconciliatio（和好）。↩︎

  162. 宣告式的赦罪，尤其是直接用直陈语气或司法判决形式施行的赦罪，似乎起源较晚。↩︎

  163. 居普良 (Cypr.)，《书信》(Epist.) LV，第15章：“Neque enim prejudicamus Domino judicaturo, quominus si penitentiam plenam et justam peccatoris invenerit tunc ratum faciat, quod a nobis fuerit hic statutum. Si vero nos aliquis poenitentiae simulatione deluserit, Deus, cui non deridetur, et qui cor hominis intuetur, de his, quae nos minus perspeximus, judicet et servorum suorum sententiam Dominus mendet.”（我们并不预断那将要施行审判的主，以致妨碍他在发现罪人的悔改完全而真实时，认可我们在此所作的决定。倘若有人假装悔改来欺骗我们，那不受轻慢、鉴察人心的上帝，必对我们未能看清的事施行审判，主也必纠正他仆人的判决。）参《书信》(Epist.) LXXV. 4，以及《论跌倒者》(De Lapsi) 第17章中的类似段落。但若教会正如居普良所承认的，可能错误地赦免不配得赦免的人，那么，她也可能错误地拒绝赦免，并错误地作出逐出教会的判决。↩︎

  164. 《哥林多前书》5:1及下。参《哥林多后书》2:5及下。↩︎

  165. 详情参§183，以及德林格 (Döllinger)《希波吕托与卡利斯托》(Hippol. and Call.)，A. 普卢默 (A. Plummer) 英译本（1876年），第92页及后。↩︎

  166. 参蒙森 (Mommsen)，《论354年的编年史家》(Über den Chronographen vom Jahr 354)，1850年；利普修斯 (Lipsius)，《罗马主教年代考》(Chronologie der Röm. Bischöfe)，第40页及后；德林格 (Döllinger)，前引书（I.c.），第332页及后；雅科比 (Jacobi)，载赫尔措格 (Herzog)《百科全书》第2版，第VI卷，第142页及后。↩︎

  167. 居普良 (Cyprian) 指控他有种种骇人的残暴行为，如掠夺寡妇孤儿，严重虐待父亲和妻子，甚至在妻子怀孕期间也是如此；并说，德修 (Decius) 的逼迫爆发时，他正将因这些及类似恶行而受审。见《书信》(Ep.) 49。↩︎

  168. 在第52封书信《致安托尼安》(Ad Antonianum) 中，他试图为自己观点的这一转变辩解。↩︎

  169. Novatiani, Novatianenses（诺瓦提安派）。↩︎

  170. Καθαροί*。↩︎

  171. 优西比乌 (Eusebius) 和希腊人称他为 Νοουάτος*，并将他与迦太基的诺瓦图 (Novatus of Carthage) 混为一谈。然而，亚历山大的狄奥尼修 (Dionysius of Alex.) 称他为 Νοουατιανός*。↩︎
